**Finland**,[^cite_note-12] officially the **Republic of Finland**,[^cite_note-13] is a [Nordic country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_country) in [Northern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Europe). It borders [Sweden](5058739.md) to the northwest, [Norway](21241.md) to the north, and [Russia](25391.md) to the east, with the [Gulf of Bothnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Bothnia) to the west and the [Gulf of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Finland) to the south, opposite [Estonia](28222445.md). Its capital and largest city is [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki). Finland has a population of 5.7 million.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] The official languages are [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language) and [Swedish](26689.md), the [mother tongues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) of 83.5 percent and 5.0 percent of the population, respectively.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1][^cite_note-14] Finland's climate varies from [humid continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) in the south to [boreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_climate) in the north. Its land is predominantly covered by [boreal forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_forest), with [over 180,000 recorded lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lakes_of_Finland).[^cite_note-thousand-15][^cite_note-travel+leisure-16]

Finland was first settled around 9000 BC after the [last Ice Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period).[^cite_note-17] During the [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age), various cultures emerged, distinguished by different styles of ceramics. The [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) and [Iron Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) were marked by contacts with other cultures in [Fennoscandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fennoscandia) and the [Baltic region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_region).[^cite_note-18] From the middle of the 13th century, Finland became part of Sweden during the [Northern Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Crusades). In 1809, as a result of the [Finnish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_War), Finland was captured from Sweden and became [an autonomous grand duchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland) within the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire). During this period, [Finnish art flourished](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Finnish_Art) and an [independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Finland) gradually developed.

Following the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) of 1917, Finland [declared its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Declaration_of_Independence). A [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Civil_War) ensued the following year, with the anticommunist [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whites_\(Finland\)) emerging victorious. Finland's status as a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) was confirmed in 1919. During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_in_World_War_II), Finland fought against the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) in the [Winter War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_War) and the [Continuation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuation_War), and later against [Nazi Germany](21212.md) in the [Lapland War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_War). As a result, it lost parts of its territory to the Soviet Union but retained its independence and democracy. During the [Cold War](325329.md), Finland embraced an official policy of neutrality. After the Cold War, Finland became a member of the [European Union](9317.md) in 1995 and the [Eurozone](184391.md) in 1999. Following the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Finland joined [NATO](21133.md) in 2023.

Finland became the first country in Europe to grant [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage#Dates_by_country) in 1906, and the first in the world to give all adult citizens the right to run for public office.[^cite_note-full_suffrage-20] Finland remained a largely [rural and agrarian country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_country) until the 1950s, when it pursued rapid industrialisation and a [Nordic-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_model) [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state), resulting in [an advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Finland) and high [per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita). The [country consistently ranks highly in international rankings across various categories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Finland), such as education, economic competitiveness, happiness, and prosperity. [Finnish foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Finland) based on its [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) status emphasizes international cooperation and partnership, which has recently shifted towards closer [ties with NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations). [Finnish cultural values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Finland), including [egalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism), [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Finland), and [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Finland), are actively promoted through [membership in multiple international forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Finland#Multilateral_relations).

## Names and etymology

Main article: [Names of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Finland)

See also: [Finn (ethnonym)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finn_\(ethnonym\))

Finland is known by two unrelated names: *Suomi* ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language) for 'Finland'), used in Finnish and, in corresponding forms, in the other [Finnic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnic_languages); and *Finland*, of [Old Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Norse) origin, used in Swedish and in most other languages. The origin of both is uncertain. Explanations deriving *Suomi* from *suo* ('mire') or *suomu* ('scale') are rejected on semantic and morphological grounds; current discussion centres on a Baltic loan etymology linking *Suomi* to the province name *[Häme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A4me)* and the Sámi endonym *[sápmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1pmi)*, and on the view that *Suomi* and *sápmi* continue a common [Finno-Samic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Samic_languages) self-designation.[^cite_note-ssa-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-deSmit-23] The Old Norse element *finnr* behind *Finland* appears to have been a Scandinavian term for northern neighbours in general, denoting the [Sámi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_people) in Norwegian usage and the inhabitants of south-western Finland in Swedish usage.[^cite_note-yle-finland-24]

In the earliest historical sources, from the 12th and 13th centuries, the term *Finland* refers to the coastal region around [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku) (Swedish: *Åbo*) in the southwest of modern Finland; this region later became known as [Finland Proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_Proper_\(historical_province\)) in distinction from the country name Finland.[^cite_note-\:0-25][^cite_note-Maillefer-26] The Latin form *Finlandia* began to appear in the titles of the [bishops of Åbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Turku) from 1221.[^cite_note-fho-finlandia-27]

In the 14th and 15th centuries, the southwest ([Eura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eura) and Turku), the interior (around [Lake Päijänne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_P%C3%A4ij%C3%A4nne)), and the southeast (reaching [Lake Ladoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Ladoga)) regions of modern Finland were incorporated into the Swedish realm; the entire area was called *Finlandia et partes orientales* by the Scandinavians, and the interior was known as [Tavastia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tavastia_\(historical_province\)).[^cite_note-Maillefer-26] From 1308 *[Österland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96sterland)* ("Eastland"), originally the territories east of Finland Proper, served as the official designation of the eastern part of the realm; from 1464, and especially after 1500, *Finland* displaced it, and *Österlandet* appeared in official documents for the last time in 1525.[^cite_note-fho-finlandia-27][^cite_note-fho-osterlandet-28] By the end of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), the term *Finland* had thus been extended to the east as well.[^cite_note-29]

## History

Main article: [History of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Finland)

### Prehistory

Main article: [History of Finland § Prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Finland#Prehistory)



Stone Age bear head gavel found in [Paltamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paltamo), Kainuu

[^cite_note-30]

[^cite_note-31]

The area that is now Finland was settled in, at the latest, around 8500 BC during the [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age) towards the end of the [last glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_glacial_period). The [artefacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artifact_\(archaeology\)) the first settlers left behind present characteristics that are shared with those found in [Estonia](28222445.md), Russia, and Norway.[^cite_note-\:1-32] The earliest people were [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer), using stone tools.[^cite_note-VF-Pre-33]

The first pottery appeared in 5200 BC, when the [Comb Ceramic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comb_Ceramic_culture) was introduced.[^cite_note-Hist-Fin-Geo-34] The area of present-day Finland was in the western limits of the culture, which produced pottery with a distinct [comb pattern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comb_Ceramic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby20062-35] The arrival of the [Corded Ware culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corded_Ware_culture) in the south of coastal Finland between 3000 and 2500 BC may have coincided with the start of agriculture.[^cite_note-Virt-Mino-36] Even with the introduction of agriculture, hunting and fishing continued to be important parts of the subsistence economy.

Based upon linguistic evidence, Finland seems to have been primarily inhabited by speakers of [Paleo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-European_languages) languages prior to the migration of the Finno-Ugric peoples, which influenced the [Sámi languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_languages) who were the first Finno-Ugric peoples to move towards Finland. These languages have been divided into [Paleo-Laplandic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Laplandic_languages) which were spoken around Lapland, and the Lakelandic languages spoken in most of modern-day Finland.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38]

In the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), permanent all-year-round cultivation and [animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry) spread, but the cold climate slowed the change.[^cite_note-\:2-39] The [Seima-Turbino phenomenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seima-Turbino_phenomenon) brought the first bronze artefacts to the region and possibly also the [Finno-Ugric languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Ugric_languages).[^cite_note-\:2-39][^cite_note-40] Commercial contacts that had so far mostly been to Estonia started to extend to Scandinavia. Domestic manufacture of bronze artefacts started 1300 BC.[^cite_note-41]

In the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age), population grew. [Finland Proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_Proper) was the most densely populated area. Commercial contacts in the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) region grew and extended during the eighth and ninth centuries. Main exports from Finland were furs, slaves, [castoreum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castoreum), and falcons to European courts. Imports included silk and other fabrics, jewelry, [Ulfberht swords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulfberht_swords), and, in lesser extent, glass. Production of iron started approximately in 500 BC.[^cite_note-42] At the end of the ninth century, indigenous artefact culture, especially weapons and women's jewelry, had more common local features than ever before. This has been interpreted to be expressing common Finnish identity.[^cite_note-43]

An early form of [Finnic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnic_languages) spread to the Baltic Sea region approximately 1900 BC. Common Finnic language was spoken around [Gulf of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Finland) 2000 years ago. The dialects from which the modern-day Finnish language was developed came into existence during the Iron Age.[^cite_note-44] Contacts with the ancient [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_peoples) and eastern [Germanic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) greatly influenced the [Proto-Finnic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Finnic_language).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby20062-35] Although distantly related, the [Sámi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_people) retained the hunter-gatherer lifestyle longer than the Finns. The Sámi cultural identity and the [Sámi languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_languages) have survived in [Lapland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_\(Finland\)), the northernmost province.

### Swedish era

Main article: [Finland under Swedish rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_under_Swedish_rule)



Now lying within Helsinki, [Suomenlinna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suomenlinna) is a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md) consisting of an inhabited 18th-century sea fortress built on six islands. It is one of Finland's most popular tourist attractions.

The 12th and 13th centuries were a violent time in the northern Baltic Sea. The [Livonian Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_Crusade) was ongoing, and the [Finnish tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_tribes), such as the [Tavastians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tavastians) and [Karelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelia_\(historical_province_of_Finland\)), were in [frequent conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Finnish_wars) with [Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Novgorod) and with each other. Also, during the 12th and 13th centuries, several crusades from the Catholic realms of the Baltic Sea area were made against the Finnish tribes. [Danes](419786.md) waged at least three crusades to Finland, in 1187 or slightly earlier,[^cite_note-\:3-45] in 1191 and in 1202,[^cite_note-46] and the [Swedes](29380512.md) waged the [Second Swedish Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Swedish_Crusade) to Finland in 1249 against the Tavastians, and the [Third Swedish Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Swedish_Crusade) to Finland in 1293 against the Karelians. The so-called [First Swedish Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Swedish_Crusade) to Finland, possibly in 1155, likely never occurred.[^cite_note-47]

As a result of the Crusades, mostly with the Second Swedish Crusade led by [Birger Jarl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birger_Jarl), and the [colonization of some Finnish coastal areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_colonisation_of_Finland) with Christian [Swedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish-speaking_population_of_Finland) during the Middle Ages,[^cite_note-48] Finland gradually became part of the kingdom of Sweden and the sphere of influence of the [Catholic Church](606848.md).[^cite_note-49] Under Sweden, Finland was annexed as part of the cultural order of [Western Europe](33800.md).[^cite_note-infoFinland.fi-50] The Swedes built fortresses in [Häme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A4me) and [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku), while a Swedish royal council was instituted, an administrative structure and fiscal apparatus was created, and law codes were codified during the reigns of [Magnus Ladulås](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnus_Ladul%C3%A5s) (1275–1290) and [Magnus Eriksson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnus_Eriksson) (1319–1364).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby20069-51] As a result, the Finnish lands were firmly integrated into the Swedish realm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby20069-51]

[Swedish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_Swedish) was the dominant language of the nobility, administration, and education; [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language) was chiefly a language for the [peasantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasant), clergy, and local [courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court) in predominantly Finnish-speaking areas.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] During the [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation), the [Finns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns) gradually converted to [Lutheranism](23371382.md).[^cite_note-54] The end of the [Kalmar Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmar_Union) ushered in an era of religious, social, and economic changes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby200628-55] [Gustav Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Vasa) (r. 1523–1560) made his second son [Johan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_III_of_Sweden) the [duke of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_of_Finland), while [Gustav Adolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustavus_Adolphus) (r. 1611–1632) created the office of [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Finland) for Finland as part of his restructuring of the administration of the Swedish realm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby200637–38-56]

In the 16th century, a bishop and Lutheran Reformer [Mikael Agricola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikael_Agricola) published the first written works in Finnish;[^cite_note-57] and Finland's current capital city, [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki), was founded by King [Gustav Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_I_of_Sweden) in 1550.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] The first university in Finland, the [Royal Academy of Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Turku), was established by Queen [Christina of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christina_of_Sweden) at the proposal of Count [Per Brahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_Brahe_the_Younger) in 1640.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61]

The Finns reaped a reputation in the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War) (1618–1648) as a well-trained [cavalrymen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalrymen) called "[Hakkapeliitta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakkapeliitta)".[^cite_note-62] Finland suffered a severe [famine in 1695–1697](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_of_Finland_\(1695%E2%80%931697\)), during which about one third of the Finnish population died,[^cite_note-empire-63] and a [devastating plague occurred a few years later](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_plague_during_the_Great_Northern_War).

In the 18th century, wars between Sweden and Russia twice led to the occupation of Finland by Russian forces, times known to the Finns as the [Greater Wrath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Wrath) (1714–1721) and the [Lesser Wrath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Swedish_War_\(1741%E2%80%931743\)) (1742–1743).[^cite_note-tif-64][^cite_note-empire-63] It is estimated that almost an entire generation of young men was lost during the Great Wrath, due mainly to the destruction of homes and farms, and the burning of Helsinki.[^cite_note-Nordstrom,_Scandinavia-65] By 1800, the population in Finland had recovered to around 833,000 people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHårdstedt2023120–121-66]

### Grand Duchy of Finland

Main article: [Grand Duchy of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland)

The Swedish era ended with the [Finnish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_War) of 1809. On 29 March 1809, after being conquered by the armies of [Alexander I of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia), Finland became [an autonomous grand duchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland) within the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), as recognised by the [Diet of Porvoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diet_of_Porvoo).[^cite_note-67] This situation continued until the end of 1917.[^cite_note-tif-64] In 1812, Alexander I incorporated the Russian [province of Vyborg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyborg_Governorate) into the Grand Duchy of Finland. In 1854, Finland became involved in Russia's involvement in the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) when the British and French navies bombed the Finnish coast and [Åland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85land) during the [Åland War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85land_War).[^cite_note-Junnila-68]



[Edvard Isto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edvard_Isto), *[The Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Attack_\(painting\))*, 1899. The [Russian eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_eagle) is attacking the [Finnish Maiden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Maiden), trying to steal her book of laws

Although Swedish was still widely spoken, the Finnish language began to gain recognition during this period. From the 1860s, a strong Finnish [nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism), known as the [Fennoman movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fennoman_movement), grew. As a leading Fennoman philosopher and statesman, [J. V. Snellman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_Vilhelm_Snellman) played a decisive role in the 19th-century Finnish national movement by championing the official status of the Finnish language and spearheading the introduction of the [Finnish markka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_markka) in 1865, thereby strengthening the autonomy of the Grand Duchy of Finland.[^cite_note-Junnila-68][^cite_note-69] Milestones included the publication of what would become Finland's [national epic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_epic), the *[Kalevala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalevala)*, in 1835 and the legal equality of the Finnish language with Swedish in 1892. In the spirit of [Adolf Ivar Arwidsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Ivar_Arwidsson) – "we are not Swedes, we do not want to become Russians, so let us be Finns" – a Finnish national identity was established.[^cite_note-70] Nevertheless, there was no real independence movement in Finland until the early 20th century.[^cite_note-a1-71]

The [Finnish famine of 1866–1868](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_famine_of_1866%E2%80%931868) occurred after freezing temperatures in early September devastated crops and killed around 15% of the population, making it one of the worst [famines](51329.md) in European history.[^cite_note-72] The famine led the Russian Empire to relax financial regulations, and investment increased in the following decades. Economic development was rapid.[^cite_note-equity-73] The [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) per capita was still half of that of the United States and a third of that of Britain.[^cite_note-equity-73]

From 1899 to 1917, the Russian Empire pursued [a policy of Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification_of_Finland), which was suspended between 1905 and 1908. In 1906, [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) was introduced in the Grand Duchy of Finland. However, relations between the Grand Duchy of Finland and the Russian Empire soured when the Russian government began to take steps to restrict Finland's special status and autonomy. For example, universal suffrage was virtually meaningless in practice, as the [tsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar) did not have to approve any of the laws passed by the Finnish parliament. The desire for independence gained ground, first among radical [liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism)[^cite_note-74] and [socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), partly driven by a declaration called th&#x65;*&#x20;[February Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Manifesto)* by the last tsar of the Russian Empire, [Nicholas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia), on 15 February 1899.[^cite_note-75]

### Civil war and early independence

Main articles: [Independence of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Finland) and [Finnish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Civil_War)



Finnish military leader and statesman [C. G. E. Mannerheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._G._E._Mannerheim) as [general officer](148537.md) leading the [White Victory Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Victory_Parade) at the end of the [Finnish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Civil_War) in Helsinki, 1918

After the [February Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Revolution) of 1917, Finland's position as a Grand Duchy under the rule of the Russian Empire was questioned. The [Finnish parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland), controlled by the [Social Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Finland), passed the so-called [Power Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Finland#Power_act) to give the parliament supreme authority. This was rejected by the [Russian Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government), which decided to dissolve the parliament.[^cite_note-76] New elections were held in which the right-wing parties won by a small majority. Some social democrats refused to accept the result, claiming that the dissolution of parliament and the subsequent elections were extra-legal. The two almost equally powerful political blocs, the right-wing parties and the Social Democratic Party, were deeply divided.

The [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) in Russia changed the geopolitical situation once again. Suddenly the right-wing parties in Finland began to reconsider their decision to block the transfer of supreme executive power from the Russian government to Finland when the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) came to power in Russia. The right-wing government, led by Prime Minister [P. E. Svinhufvud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pehr_Evind_Svinhufvud), presented the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Declaration_of_Independence) on 4 December 1917, which was officially approved by the Finnish Parliament on 6 December. The [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) (RSFSR), led by [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) was the first country to recognise Finland's independence on 4 January 1918.[^cite_note-77]

On 27 January 1918, the government began to disarm the Russian forces in [Ostrobothnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostrobothnia_\(historical_province\)). The socialists took control of southern Finland and Helsinki, but the white government continued in exile in [Vaasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaasa).[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] This led to a short but bitter [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Civil_War). The [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Guard_\(Finland\)), backed by [Imperial Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire), prevailed over the [Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Guards_\(Finland\))[^cite_note-80] and their self-proclaimed [Finnish Socialist Workers' Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Socialist_Workers'_Republic).[^cite_note-81] After the war, tens of thousands of Reds were interned in camps where thousands were executed or died of malnutrition and disease. A deep social and political enmity was sown between the Reds and the Whites that would last until the [Winter War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_War) and beyond.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] The civil war and the activist expeditions to Soviet Russia in 1918–1920, known as the "[Kinship Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heimosodat)", strained relations with the East.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85]



[Helsinki Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Olympic_Stadium) in 1938

After a [brief experiment with monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Finland_\(1918\)), when an attempt to make [Prince Frederick Charles of Hesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Frederick_Charles_of_Hesse) the king of Finland failed, a [republican constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Finland) was adopted and Finland became a [presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_republic), with [K. J. Ståhlberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._J._St%C3%A5hlberg) elected as its first president on 25 July 1919.[^cite_note-86] A [liberal nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalism) with a legal background, Ståhlberg anchored the state in [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy), promoted the [rule of law](25166191.md) and initiated internal reforms.[^cite_note-87] Finland was also one of the first European countries to strongly promote [women's equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights), with [Miina Sillanpää](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miina_Sillanp%C3%A4%C3%A4) becoming the first female minister in Finnish history in [Väinö Tanner's cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanner_Cabinet) in 1926–1927.[^cite_note-88] The Finnish-Russian border was established in 1920 by the [Treaty of Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tartu_\(Finland%E2%80%93Russia\)), which largely followed the historical border but gave Finland [Pechenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechengsky_District) ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language): *Petsamo*) and its [Barents Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barents_Sea) port.[^cite_note-tif-64] Finnish democracy survived Soviet coup attempts and the anti-communist [Lapua movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapua_movement).

In 1917, there were three million people in the country. After the civil war, a credit-based [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform) was introduced, increasing the proportion of the population with access to capital.[^cite_note-equity-73] About 70% of the workforce was employed in agriculture and 10% in industry.[^cite_note-89]

### World War II

Main article: [Finland in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_in_World_War_II)



Finnish troops [raise a flag on the three-country cairn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raising_the_Flag_on_the_Three-Country_Cairn) in April 1945 at the close of the [World War II in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Finland_during_World_War_II).

The [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and [Nazi Germany](21212.md) signed the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact) on 23 August 1939, which divided Europe into spheres of influence between the two dictatorships.[^cite_note-90] In accordance with the pact, the Soviet Union launched the [Winter War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_War) on 30 November 1939 in order to annex Finland.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] The [Finnish Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Democratic_Republic) was set up by [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) at the beginning of the war to govern Finland after Soviet conquest.[^cite_note-Tannerv-93] There was widespread international condemnation of the unprovoked attack and it led to the Soviet Union being expelled from the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations).[^cite_note-94] The [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) was defeated in numerous battles, most notably the [Battle of Suomussalmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Suomussalmi).[^cite_note-95] After two months of negligible progress on the battlefield, as well as heavy losses in men and material,[^cite_note-96] Soviet forces began to advance in February and [reached Vyborg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vyborg_Bay_\(1940\)) ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language): *Viipuri*) in March. The [Moscow Peace Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Peace_Treaty) was signed on 12 March 1940, and the war ended the following day. Finland had defended its independence, but ceded 9% of its territory to the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-\:4-98] Between 1939 and 1944, some 400,000 people were [evacuated from Karelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_of_Finnish_Karelia).



Areas ceded by Finland to the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) during [World War II](32927.md). The [Porkkala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porkkala) land lease was returned to Finland in 1956.

Hostilities resumed in June 1941 with the [Continuation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuation_War), when Finland allied itself with Germany following the [latter's invasion of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa); the main aim was to regain the territory lost to the Soviets barely a year earlier.[^cite_note-99] Finnish troops [occupied Eastern Karelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_military_administration_in_Eastern_Karelia) from 1941 to 1944 and assisted the German Army in the [Siege of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leningrad).[^cite_note-100] The massive Soviet [Vyborg–Petrozavodsk offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyborg%E2%80%93Petrozavodsk_offensive) in the summer of 1944 led to a breakthrough until the Finns finally repulsed it at [Tali–Ihantala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tali%E2%80%93Ihantala). This partial Soviet success led to a stalemate and later an [armistice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Armistice). This was followed by the [Lapland War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_War) of 1944–1945, when Finland fought retreating German forces in northern Finland.

The [armistice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Armistice) and [treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Soviet_Treaty_of_1948) signed with the Soviet Union in 1944 and 1948 included Finnish obligations, restraints, and reparations, as well as further territorial concessions. As a result of the two wars, Finland lost 12% of its land area, 20% of its industrial capacity, its second largest city, [Vyborg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyborg) ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language): *Viipuri*), and the ice-free port of [Liinakhamari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liinakhamari) ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language): *Liinahamari*).[^cite_note-\:4-98] The Finns lost around 97,000 soldiers and were forced to pay war reparations initially set at \$300 million in 1938 prices, later adjusted to \$226.5 million.[^cite_note-101] However, the country avoided occupation by Soviet forces and managed to retain its independence. Along with [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain), Finland emerged from the war as one of the only European countries to have taken part in hostilities that was never occupied and managed to preserve its democracy throughout.[^cite_note-102]

In the decades following World War II, the [Communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Finland) were a strong political party. Furthermore, the Soviet Union persuaded Finland to refuse [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan) aid. However, in the hope of preserving Finland's independence, the United States provided secret development aid and supported the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Finland).[^cite_note-103]

### After the war



[Alvar Aalto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvar_Aalto)'s [Finlandia Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandia_Hall) hosted the [Conference on Security and Co-operation in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_on_Security_and_Co-operation_in_Europe) in 1975.

The development of trade with the Western powers, such as the United Kingdom, and the payment of reparations to the Soviet Union led to Finland's transformation from a primarily [agrarian society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_society) to an industrialised one. [Valmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valmet), originally a shipyard and then several metal workshops, was established to produce materials for war reparations. After the reparations were paid, Finland continued to trade with the Soviet Union as part of [bilateral trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilateral_trade).

In 1950, 46% of Finnish workers were employed in agriculture and a third lived in urban areas, but new jobs in manufacturing, services and trade quickly attracted people to the cities.[^cite_note-populationdevelopment-104] The average number of births per woman fell from a [baby boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_boom) peak of 3.5 in 1947 to 1.5 in 1973. As the baby boomers entered the workforce, the economy failed to create jobs fast enough and hundreds of thousands emigrated to more industrialised Sweden, with emigration peaking in 1969 and 1970.[^cite_note-populationdevelopment-104] Finland participated in trade liberalisation in the [World Bank](36752.md), the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) and the [General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Tariffs_and_Trade).

During the [Cold War](325329.md), Finland officially embraced a policy of [neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country). The [YYA Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Soviet_Treaty_of_1948) (Finno-Soviet Pact of Friendship, Cooperation and Mutual Assistance) recognized Finland's desire to remain outside great-power conflicts. From 1956 president [Urho Kekkonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urho_Kekkonen) had a virtual monopoly on relations with the Soviet Union, which was crucial to his continued popularity. In politics, there was a tendency to avoid any policy or statement that could be interpreted as anti-Soviet. This phenomenon was dubbed "[Finlandisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandisation)" by the West German press.[^cite_note-105]

A [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) was maintained in Finland. Various industries benefited from trade privileges with the Soviets. Economic growth was rapid in the post-war period, and by 1975 Finland's GDP per capita was the 15th highest in the world. During the 1970s and 1980s, Finland built one of the most extensive [welfare states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) in the world. In 1973, Finland negotiated a treaty with the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community) (EEC) that reduced tariffs, enhancing trade relations.[^cite_note-106]

Miscalculated macroeconomic decisions, a [banking crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_banking_crisis_of_1990s), the collapse of its largest trading partner, the Soviet Union, and a global economic downturn caused a deep recession in Finland in the [early 1990s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_1990s_depression_in_Finland). The recession bottomed out in 1993 and Finland enjoyed more than a decade of steady economic growth.[^cite_note-107] After the collapse of the Soviet Union, Finland began to integrate more closely with the West.[^cite_note-108] Finland [joined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_enlargement_of_the_European_Union) the [European Union](9317.md) in 1995 and the [euro zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_zone) in 1999. Much of the economic growth of the late 1990s was fuelled by the success of mobile phone manufacturer [Nokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia).[^cite_note-infoFinland.fi-50][^cite_note-109]

### 21st century



Prime Minister [Sanna Marin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanna_Marin) and President [Sauli Niinistö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauli_Niinist%C3%B6) at the press conference announcing Finland's intent to apply to NATO on 2022

The Finnish people elected [Tarja Halonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarja_Halonen) in the [2000 Presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Finnish_presidential_election), making her the first female President of Finland.[^cite_note-110] Her predecessor, President [Martti Ahtisaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martti_Ahtisaari), later won the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) in 2008. The [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) paralysed  Finland's exports in 2008, leading to weaker economic growth throughout the decade.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] [Sauli Niinistö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauli_Niinist%C3%B6) was elected President of Finland from 2012 until 2024, when [Alexander Stubb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Stubb) took over.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114]

Finnish support for [NATO](21133.md) rose sharply after the [Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022](79400268.md). Before February 2022, opinion polls showed a narrow but decisive majority against NATO membership;[^cite_note-115] by April, a supermajority was in favour of membership.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] On 11 May 2022, Finland signed a mutual security pact with the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-120] On 12 May, Finland's president and Prime Minister called for NATO membership "without delay".[^cite_note-121] Subsequently, on 17 May, the Finnish Parliament voted 188–8 in favour of Finland's accession to NATO.[^cite_note-122] [Finland became a member of NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations) on 4 April 2023.[^cite_note-finland-and-nato-123]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Finland)

See also: [List of cities and towns in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Finland), [List of lakes of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lakes_of_Finland), [List of national parks of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Finland), and [Environmental issues in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Finland)

Finland is often referred as "the land of a thousand lakes".

[^cite_note-thousand-15]

&#x20;Picture of [Lake Pielinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pielinen) in [North Karelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Karelia).



[Saana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saana) fell in [Kilpisjärvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilpisj%C3%A4rvi), Finnish Lapland.

Lying approximately between latitudes [60°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60th_parallel_north) and [70° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/70th_parallel_north), and longitudes [20°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_meridian_east) and [32° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_meridian_east), Finland is one of the world's northernmost countries. Of world capitals, only [Reykjavík](25798.md) lies more to the north than Helsinki. The country is geographically stretched strongly in a north–south direction: the distance from the southernmost point – [Hanko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanko,_Finland) in Uusimaa – to the northernmost – [Nuorgam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuorgam) in Lapland – is 1,160 kilometres (720 mi).

Finland has about 168,000 lakes (of area larger than 500 m<sup>2</sup> or 0.12 acres) and 179,000 islands.[^cite_note-124] Its largest lake, [Saimaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saimaa), is the fourth largest in Europe. The [Finnish Lakeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Lakeland) is the area with the most lakes in the country;[^cite_note-travel+leisure-16] many of the major cities in the area, most notably [Tampere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere), [Jyväskylä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyv%C3%A4skyl%C3%A4) and [Kuopio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuopio), are located near the large lakes. Lying off the coast of Finland is the world's largest archipelago, a formation consisting of more than 50,000 islands. The greatest concentration of these islands is found in the southwest in a part of the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) called the [Archipelago Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago_Sea), between continental Finland and the island of [Åland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85land).[^cite_note-125]

Much of the geography of Finland is a result of the Ice Age. The glaciers were thicker and lasted longer in [Fennoscandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fennoscandia) compared with the rest of Europe. The eroding effects have contributed to a mostly flat landscape in Finland, characterized by hills. However, in the northern regions, including areas bordering the [Scandinavian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Mountains), the terrain features mountainous elevations. At 1,324 metres (4,344 ft), [Halti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halti) is the highest point in Finland. It is found in the north of Lapland at the border between Finland and Norway. The highest mountain whose peak is entirely in Finland is [Ridnitšohkka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridnit%C5%A1ohkka) at 1,316 m (4,318 ft), directly adjacent to Halti.

The retreating glaciers have left the land with [terminal moraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_moraine) deposits. These are ridges of stratified gravel and sand, running northwest to southeast, where the ancient edge of the glacier once lay. Among the biggest of these are the three [Salpausselkä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salpausselk%C3%A4) ridges that run across southern Finland. [Eskers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskers) are also found.[^cite_note-126]

Having been compressed under the enormous weight of the glaciers, terrain in Finland is rising due to the [post-glacial rebound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-glacial_rebound). The effect is strongest around the Gulf of Bothnia, where land steadily rises about 1 cm (0.4 in) a year. As a result, the old sea bottom turns little by little into dry land: the surface area of the country is expanding by about 7 square kilometres (2.7 sq mi) annually.[^cite_note-127] Relatively speaking, Finland is rising from the sea.[^cite_note-EB-128]

The landscape is covered mostly by coniferous [taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga) forests and [fens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fen), with little cultivated land. Of the total area, 10% is lakes, rivers, and ponds, and 78% is forest. The forest consists of [pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine), [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce), [birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birch), and other species.[^cite_note-129] Finland is the largest producer of wood in Europe and among the largest in the world. The most common type of rock is [granite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granite). It is a ubiquitous part of the scenery, visible wherever there is no soil cover. Moraine or [till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Till) is the most common type of soil, covered by a thin layer of [humus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humus) of biological origin. [Podzol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podzol) profile development is seen in most forest soils except where drainage is poor. [Gleysols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gleysol) and peat [bogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bog) occupy poorly drained areas.

### Biodiversity

Main articles: [Fauna of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Finland) and [Wildlife of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Finland)



In Finland, reindeer graze in [Lapland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_\(Finland\)) area and on the [fells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fells).



The [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear) is Finland's national animal.

[^cite_note-130]

&#x20;It is also the largest [carnivore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivore) in Finland.

[Phytogeographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytogeography), Finland is shared between the Arctic, central European, and northern European provinces of the [Circumboreal Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumboreal_Region) within the [Boreal Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_Kingdom). According to the [WWF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Fund_for_Nature), the territory of Finland can be subdivided into three [ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion): the [Scandinavian and Russian taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_and_Russian_taiga), [Sarmatic mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatic_mixed_forests), and [Scandinavian Montane Birch forest and grasslands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Montane_Birch_forest_and_grasslands).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-131] Taiga covers most of Finland from northern regions of southern provinces to the north of Lapland. On the southwestern coast, south of the Helsinki-[Rauma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rauma,_Finland) line, forests are characterized by mixed forests, that are more typical in the Baltic region. In the extreme north of Finland, near the [tree line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_line) and Arctic Ocean, Montane Birch forests are common. Finland had a 2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 5.08/10, ranking it 109th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-132]

Similarly, Finland has a diverse and extensive range of fauna. There are at least sixty native [mammalian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal) species, 248 breeding bird species, over 70 fish species, and 11 reptile and frog species present today, many migrating from neighbouring countries thousands of years ago. Large and widely recognized wildlife mammals found in Finland are the [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear), [grey wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_wolf), [wolverine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolverine), [Eurasian lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_lynx), [European roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer), [reindeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reindeer) and [elk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose). Three of the more striking birds are the [whooper swan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whooper_swan), a large European swan and the national bird of Finland; the [Western capercaillie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_capercaillie), a large, black-plumaged member of the [grouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grouse) family; and the [Eurasian eagle-owl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_eagle-owl). The latter is considered an indicator of [old-growth forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest) connectivity, and has been declining because of landscape fragmentation.[^cite_note-133] Around 24,000 species of insects are prevalent in Finland some of the most common being [hornets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornets) with tribes of [beetles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beetles) such as the [Onciderini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onciderini) also being common. The most common breeding birds are the [willow warbler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willow_warbler), [common chaffinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_chaffinch), and [redwing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redwing).[^cite_note-134] Of some seventy species of freshwater fish, the [northern pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_pike), [perch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perch), and others are plentiful. [Atlantic salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_salmon) remains the favourite of [fly rod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly_fishing) enthusiasts.

The endangered [Saimaa ringed seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saimaa_ringed_seal), one of only three lake seal species in the world, exists only in the [Saimaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saimaa) lake system of southeastern Finland, down to only 390 seals today.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] The species has become the emblem of the Finnish Association for Nature Conservation.[^cite_note-137]

A third of Finland's land area originally consisted of [moorland](553383.md), about half of this area has been drained for cultivation over the past centuries.[^cite_note-138]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Finland)



[Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) types of Finland

The main factor influencing Finland's climate is the country's geographical position between the [60th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60th_parallel_north) and [70th northern parallels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/70th_parallel_north) in the [Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) continent's coastal zone. In the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), the largest part of Finland lies in the [boreal zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate), characterized by warm summers and freezing winters. Within the country, the [temperateness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperateness) varies considerably between the southern coastal regions and the extreme north, showing characteristics of both a [maritime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) and a [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate). Finland is near enough to the Atlantic Ocean to be continuously warmed by the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream). The Gulf Stream combines with the moderating effects of the Baltic Sea and numerous inland lakes to explain the unusually warm climate compared with other regions that share the same [latitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latitude), such as [Alaska](624.md), [Siberia](29639.md), and southern [Greenland](12118.md).[^cite_note-Finland's_climate-139]



Share of forest area in total land area, top countries (2021). Finland has the tenth highest percentage of forest cover in the world.

Winters in southern Finland (when the mean daily temperature remains below 0 °C or 32 °F) are usually about 100 days long, and in the inland the snow typically covers the land from about late November to April, and on the coastal areas such as Helsinki, snow often covers the land from late December to late March.[^cite_note-140] Even in the south, the harshest winter nights can see the temperatures fall to −30 °C (−22 °F), although on coastal areas like Helsinki, temperatures below −30 °C (−22 °F) are rare. Climatic summers (when mean daily temperature remains above 10 °C or 50 °F) in southern Finland last from about late May to mid-September, and in the inland, the warmest days of July can reach over 35 °C (95 °F).[^cite_note-Finland's_climate-139] Although most of Finland lies on the [taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga) belt, the southernmost coastal regions are sometimes classified as [hemiboreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemiboreal).[^cite_note-Havas-141]

In northern Finland, particularly in Lapland, the winters are long and cold, while the summers are relatively warm but short. On the most severe winter days in Lapland can see the temperature fall to −45 °C (−49 °F). The winter of the north lasts for about 200 days with permanent snow cover from about mid-October to early May. Summers in the north are quite short, only two to three months, but can still see maximum daily temperatures above 25 °C (77 °F) during heat waves.[^cite_note-Finland's_climate-139] No part of Finland has [Arctic tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_tundra), but [Alpine tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_tundra) can be found at the [fells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fell) Lapland.[^cite_note-Havas-141]

The Finnish climate is suitable for cereal farming only in the southernmost regions, while the northern regions are suitable for [animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry).[^cite_note-142]

A quarter of Finland's territory lies within the [Arctic Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Circle) and the [midnight sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_sun) can be experienced for more days the farther north one travels. At Finland's northernmost point, the sun does not set for 73 consecutive days during summer and does not rise at all for 51 days during winter.[^cite_note-Finland's_climate-139]

Finland is ranked 4th in [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index) for year 2024.[^cite_note-143] This Index combines various indicators around known issues around the world and measures how good they fit in among each countries on a scale. Finland scores good in parameters like Climate Change Mitigation, Waste Management, Air pollution, Air quality etc.[^cite_note-144]

## Government and politics

Main article: [Politics of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Finland)



[Alexander Stubb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Stubb)\
[President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Finland)



[Petteri Orpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petteri_Orpo)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland)

&#x20;Modern Finnish values are rooted in [egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism) ideals.[^cite_note-\:5-145] The country maintains a [Nordic welfare model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_model) with [significant state intervention in critical economic sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Finland).[^cite_note-nordicmodel-146] Robust [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Finland),[^cite_note-147] strong efforts to eliminate poverty,[^cite_note-\:5-145] broad protections for [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Finland),[^cite_note-148] [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism),[^cite_note-149] and [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism) are indicators of its political and cultural values.[^cite_note-150] According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Finland performs in the high range on overall democratic measures, with particular strengths in political participation, including inclusive freedom of movement, freedom of movement and elected government.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153]



Finland is a member of:\
&#x20;  the [Eurozone](184391.md)\
&#x20;  the [European Union](9317.md)

### Constitution

The [Constitution of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Finland) defines the political system; Finland is a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) within the framework of a [representative democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy). The [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland) is the country's most powerful person. Citizens can run and vote in parliamentary, municipal, presidential, and [European Union elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_the_European_Union).

### President

Main article: [President of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Finland)

Finland's [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) is the [President of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Finland). Finland had, for most of its independence a [semi-presidential system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) of government, but in the last few decades the powers of the president have become more circumscribed, and consequently the country is now considered a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic).[^cite_note-Parliamentary-3] A new constitution, enacted in 2000, made the presidency primarily a ceremonial office. The president appoints the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland) as elected by [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland), appoints and dismisses the other ministers of the [Finnish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Government) on the recommendation of the prime minister, opens parliamentary sessions, and confers state honors. Nevertheless, the president remains responsible for Finland's [foreign relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Finland), including the making of war and peace, but excluding matters related to the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-154] Moreover, the president exercises supreme command over the [Finnish Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Defence_Forces) as commander-in-chief. In the exercise of his or her foreign and defense powers, the president is required to consult the [Finnish government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_government), but the government's advice is not binding. In addition, the president has several domestic [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_powers), including the authority to veto legislation, to grant pardons, and to appoint several public officials.[^cite_note-155] The president is also required by the Constitution to dismiss individual ministers or the entire government upon a parliamentary vote of no confidence.[^cite_note-156]

The president is directly elected via [runoff voting](25932.md) and may serve for a maximum of two consecutive 6-year terms. The current president is [Alexander Stubb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Stubb), who took office on [1 March 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Finnish_presidential_election). His predecessors were [Kaarlo Juho Ståhlberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaarlo_Juho_St%C3%A5hlberg) (1919–1925), [Lauri Kristian Relander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauri_Kristian_Relander) (1925–1931), [Pehr Evind Svinhufvud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pehr_Evind_Svinhufvud) (1931–1937), [Kyösti Kallio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ky%C3%B6sti_Kallio) (1937–1940), [Risto Ryti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risto_Ryti) (1940–1944), [Carl Gustaf Emil Mannerheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Gustaf_Emil_Mannerheim) (1944–1946), [Juho Kusti Paasikivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juho_Kusti_Paasikivi) (1946–1956), [Urho Kekkonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urho_Kekkonen) (1956–1982), [Mauno Koivisto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauno_Koivisto) (1982–1994), [Martti Ahtisaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martti_Ahtisaari) (1994–2000), [Tarja Halonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarja_Halonen) (2000–2012), and [Sauli Niinistö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauli_Niinist%C3%B6) (2012–2024).

### Parliament

Main article: [Parliament of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland)



The [Parliament of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland)'s main building in Helsinki



The Session Hall of the Parliament of Finland

The 200-member [unicameral](204304.md) Parliament of Finland ([Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language): *Eduskunta*) exercises supreme legislative authority in the country. It may alter the constitution and ordinary laws, dismiss the cabinet, and override presidential vetoes. Its acts are not subject to judicial review; the constitutionality of new laws is assessed by the parliament's [constitutional law committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland#Committees). The parliament is elected for a term of four years using the proportional [D'Hondt method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D'Hondt_method) within several multi-seat constituencies through the [most open list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_list#Most_open) multi-member districts. Various parliament committees listen to experts and prepare legislation.

Significant [parliamentary parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Finland) are [Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Finland\)), [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Finland\)), [Finns Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party), [Green League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_League), [Left Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left_Alliance_\(Finland\)), [National Coalition Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Coalition_Party), [Social Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Finland) and [Swedish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_People's_Party_of_Finland).

### Cabinet

Main article: [Finnish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Government)

See also: [Prime Minister of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland)

After parliamentary elections, the parties negotiate among themselves on forming a new cabinet (the [Finnish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Government)), which then has to be approved by a simple majority vote in the parliament. The cabinet can be dismissed by a parliamentary vote of no confidence, although this rarely happens, as the parties represented in the cabinet usually make up a majority in the parliament.

The cabinet exercises most executive powers and originates most of the bills that the parliament then debates and votes on. It is headed by the [Prime Minister of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland), and consists of him or her, other ministers, and the [Chancellor of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_Justice_\(Finland\)). Each minister heads his or her ministry, or, in some cases, has responsibility for a subset of a ministry's policy. After the prime minister, the most powerful minister is often the [minister of finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_\(Finland\)).

As no one party ever dominates the parliament, Finnish cabinets are multi-party coalitions. As a rule, the post of prime minister goes to the leader of the biggest party and that of the minister of finance to the leader of the second biggest.

The [Orpo Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orpo_Cabinet) is the incumbent 77th government of Finland. It took office on 20 June 2023. The cabinet is headed by [Petteri Orpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petteri_Orpo) and is a coalition between the National Coalition Party, Finns Party, the Swedish People's Party, and the Christian Democrats.[^cite_note-157]

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Finland)



[Municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Finland) (thin borders) and [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Finland) (thick borders) of Finland (2021)

The local administrative divisions of the country are the [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Finland) (*kunta*, *kommun*), which may also call themselves cities (*kaupunki*, *stad*). As of 2021, there are 309 municipalities.[^cite_note-Suomen_Kuntaliitto-158] Most are small with median number of 6,000 residents. The highest decision-making authority is the municipal council, whose members are elected for a four-year term. The municipal elections, which are based on a [partisan list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partisan_\(politics\)), provide a democratic counterbalance for the political composition of the parliament. The municipalities account for half of the public spending, financed by municipal income tax, state subsidies, and other revenue. The main duties of the municipalities include education and [child care services](389615.md); cultural, youth, library and sports services; urban planning and land use; water and waste management and environmental services.[^cite_note-c008-159]

The 2023 reform transformed the administrative system from a two-tier structure (central government and municipal level) into a three-tier system. The 21 [wellbeing services counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellbeing_services_counties_of_Finland) occupy the middle level as regional democratic self-governing entities. They are responsible for organising [health](60976.md), [social welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_services) and [emergency services](146330.md). Wellbeing services counties do not have the right to levy taxes and their funding is based on central government funding.[^cite_note-stm-wellbeing-160] The City of Helsinki and the region of Åland remain outside the wellbeing services county structure.[^cite_note-c008-159]

The municipalities in mainland Finland are grouped into 18 [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Finland) (*maakunta*, *landskap*). The regions are governed by regional councils, which serve as forums of cooperation for the municipalities. They are responsible for regional development and regional land-use planning, and the coordination of [EU structural funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Structural_and_Investment_Funds).[^cite_note-c008-159] The autonomous province of Åland forms its own region, with a democratically elected [regional parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_%C3%85land) (*lagting*).[^cite_note-161]

The central government has a regional presence through which its ministries and central agencies operate. These agencies are distinct from the self-governing regional level. Since the beginning of 2026, the regional state administration has been organized into ten [Economic Development Centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Development_Centre?action=edit\&redlink=1) (*Elinvoimakeskus*). The jurisdiction of each Economic Development Centre covers one or more regions. The centres have a broad range of responsibilities, including business and industry, employment, immigration and integration, road maintenance, and biodiversity promotion.[^cite_note-162]

Sámi people have a semi-autonomous [Sámi native region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sami_native_region_\(Finland\)) in Lapland for issues on language and culture.[^cite_note-163]
