### Law

Main articles: [Law of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Finland) and [Judicial system of Finland](1728243.md)



The Court House of the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Finland)

The judicial system of Finland is a [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) system divided between [courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court) with regular civil and criminal jurisdiction and [administrative courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_court) with jurisdiction over litigation between individuals and the public administration. Finnish law is codified and based on [Swedish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_Sweden) and in a wider sense, civil law or [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law). The court system for civil and criminal jurisdiction consists of local courts, [regional appellate courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hovr%C3%A4tt), and the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Finland). The administrative branch of justice consists of administrative courts and the [Supreme Administrative Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Administrative_Court_of_Finland). In addition to the regular courts, there are a few special courts in certain branches of administration. There is also a [High Court of Impeachment](1728243.md#High_Court_of_Impeachment) for criminal charges against certain high-ranking officeholders.

Around 92% of residents have confidence in Finland's security institutions.[^cite_note-corruptionpolicing-166] The overall [crime rate of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Finland) is not high in the EU context. Some crime types are above average, notably the high [homicide](123451.md) rate for Western Europe.[^cite_note-167] A [day fine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_fine) system is in effect and also applied to offenses such as [speeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speeding). Finland has a very low number of corruption charges; [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) ranks Finland as one of the least corrupt countries in Europe.

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Finland)



[Martti Ahtisaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martti_Ahtisaari) receiving the Nobel Peace Prize in 2008

Finland's middle power status includes a strong economy, international cooperation, and commitment to human rights.[^cite_note-f494-168] According to the 2012 constitution, the president leads foreign policy in cooperation with the government, except that the president has no role in EU affairs.[^cite_note-Finnish_constitution-169] In 2008, president [Martti Ahtisaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martti_Ahtisaari) was awarded the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md).[^cite_note-Nobelprize-170]

Finland's [relationship with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93Russia_relations) deteriorated following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), with a number of Russian diplomats expelled for spying, Russians restricted from visiting Finland and the general opinion immediately changing for [Finland to join NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations),[^cite_note-171] while it has also had a significant impact on the increasing strength of [relations between the United States and Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93United_States_relations).[^cite_note-172] According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), Finland is the 13th most peaceful country in the world.[^cite_note-173]

### Military

Main articles: [Finnish Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Defence_Forces), [Military history of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Finland), and [Finland–NATO relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations)

See also: [List of wars involving Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Finland) and [Finland–Russia border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93Russia_border)



[Sisu Nasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisu_Nasu) NA-110 tracked transport vehicle of the Finnish Army. Most conscripts receive training for warfare in winter, and transport vehicles such as this give mobility in heavy snow.

The [Finnish Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Defence_Forces) consists of an [army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Army), [navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Navy), and [air force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Air_Force). The [border guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Border_Guard), while under the Ministry of the Interior, can be incorporated into the Defence Forces during wartime. Finland maintains universal male [conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Finland), under which all male Finnish nationals above 18 years of age serve for 6 to 12 months of armed service or 12 months of [civilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siviilipalvelus) (non-armed) service. The Finnish military comprises a [cadre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadre_\(military\)) of professional soldiers (mainly officers and technical personnel), conscripts, and a large number of reservists. The standard readiness strength is 34,700 people in uniform, of which 25% are professional soldiers; the entire reserve comprises approximately 900,000 people.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-cnn-175] Voluntary post-conscription overseas peacekeeping service is popular, and troops serve around the world in UN, NATO, and EU missions. Women are allowed to serve in all combat arms; in 2022, 1,211 women entered voluntary military service.[^cite_note-176]

Finnish defence expenditure per capita is one of the highest in the European Union.[^cite_note-177] With a high capability of [military personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_personnel),[^cite_note-178] [arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal)[^cite_note-179] and [homeland defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_defence) willingness, Finland is one of Europe's militarily strongest countries.[^cite_note-cnn-175][^cite_note-180] The NATO organization will site a command unit at [Riihimäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riihim%C3%A4ki) in 2027.[^cite_note-181]

Finland became a member of [NATO](21133.md) on 4 April 2023,[^cite_note-finland-and-nato-123] though it had previously participated in the [NATO Response Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Response_Force). Finland is actively involved in multilateral military operations, including the [Joint Expeditionary Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Expeditionary_Force) (JEF),[^cite_note-182] the [EU Battlegroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Battlegroup),[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-eduskunta.fi-185] and the [Kosovo Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_Force); it also contributed to the [International Security Assistance Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] On 18 December 2023, Finland signed the [Defense Cooperation Agreement (DCA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreement_on_Defense_Cooperation_between_Finland_and_the_United_States_of_America) with the United States, which regulates the presence of the U.S. armed forces and their dependents on Finnish territory and the presence and activities of U.S. defence suppliers.[^cite_note-tbo-188]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Finland)



People gathering at the [Senate Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Square,_Helsinki), [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki), before the [2022 Helsinki Pride parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Pride) started

Finland has one of the world's most extensive [welfare systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_in_Finland), one that guarantees decent living conditions for all residents. The welfare system was created almost entirely during the first three decades after World War II.[^cite_note-LOC-189]

Section 6 of the [Finnish Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Constitution) states: "No one shall be placed in a different position on situation of sex, age, origin, language, religion, belief, opinion, state of health, disability or any other personal reason without an acceptable reason".[^cite_note-190]

Finland has been ranked above average among the world's countries in [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy),[^cite_note-191] [press freedom](105323.md),[^cite_note-192] and [human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_development_\(economics\)).[^cite_note-193] [Amnesty International](18947898.md) has expressed concern regarding some issues in Finland, such as the imprisonment of [conscientious objectors](83003.md), and [societal discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_Finland) against [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) and members of other ethnic and linguistic minorities.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

In the report of the European umbrella organization [ILGA-Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ILGA-Europe) published in May 2023, Finland [ranked sixth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Finland) in a European comparison of [LGBTQ+ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_by_country_or_territory).[^cite_note-196]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Finland)

See also: [List of companies of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_Finland), [List of largest companies in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_companies_in_Finland), and [Helsinki Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Stock_Exchange)

As of 2022, Finland ranked 16th globally in [nominal GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) according to the [IMF](15251.md). Additionally, Finland boasts a well-developed [welfare system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) that encompasses free education and universal healthcare, contributing to its reputation as a wealthy nation.

In 2018, the [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_sector) constituted the largest segment of the economy, amounting to 66% of the GDP, while manufacturing and refining made up 31%. [Primary production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector_of_the_economy) accounted for 2.9% of the economy.[^cite_note-197] Manufacturing is the primary economic sector concerning [foreign trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade).[^cite_note-198] The predominant industrial sectors in 2007 were electronics (22%), machinery, vehicles, and other engineered metal products (21.1%), forest industry (13%), and chemicals (11%). The gross domestic product reached its peak in 2021.[^cite_note-statista-199] Finland has been ranked seventh in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201]



[Kittilä Gold Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kittil%C3%A4_mine), in [Kittilä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kittil%C3%A4), [Finnish Lapland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_\(Finland\)), is the largest primary gold producer in Europe.

[^cite_note-202]

[^cite_note-203]

Finland has considerable [timber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber), mineral (including [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron), [chromium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromium), [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper), [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel), and [gold](12240.md)) and freshwater resources. Finland's gold production in 2015 was 9 metric tons.[^cite_note-204] For the rural population, [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry), [paper mills](417020.md) and agriculture are important. The [Helsinki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_metropolitan_area) accounts for roughly one-third of Finland's GDP. Private services represent the largest employer in Finland.

Finland's soil and [climate](#climate) pose particular challenges for crop production, with harsh winters and relatively short growing seasons, often interrupted by frost. However, the prevalence of the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream) and the [North Atlantic Drift Current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Drift_Current) in Finland's temperate climate allows for half of the world's arable land north of the 60° north latitude. Although annual precipitation is generally adequate, it mostly transpires during winter, which poses a continuous risk of summer droughts. Farmers have adapted to the climate by relying on quick-ripening and frost-resistant crop varieties. They cultivate south-facing slopes and rich bottomlands to ensure year-round production, even during summer frosts. Drainage systems are often utilized to remove excess water. Finland's agricultural sector has demonstrated remarkable efficiency and productivity, particularly in comparison to its European counterparts.[^cite_note-LOC-189]

Forests are crucial to the nation's economy, making it one of the world's foremost wood producers and offering raw materials at competitive prices to the [wood processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_processing) industries. The government has played an important role in forestry for a considerable period similar to that in agriculture. It has regulated tree cutting, sponsored technical improvements, and established long-term plans to guarantee the sustainability of the country's forests in supplying the wood-processing industries.[^cite_note-LOC-189]

As of 2008, the average level of income, adjusted for purchasing power, was comparable to that of Italy, Sweden, Germany and France.[^cite_note-incomecomparison-205] In 2006, 62% of the labour force was employed by firms with fewer than 250 workers, which generated 49% of total business revenue.[^cite_note-206] The employment rate of women is high. Gender segregation between male-dominated professions and female-dominated professions is higher than in the US.[^cite_note-niels-207] The proportion of part-time workers was one of the lowest in OECD in 1999.[^cite_note-niels-207] As of 2013, the 10 largest private sector employers in Finland were [Itella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itella), [Nokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia), [OP-Pohjola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OP_Financial_Group), [ISS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISS_A/S), [VR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VR_\(company\)), [Kesko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesko), [UPM-Kymmene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UPM-Kymmene), [YIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YIT), [Metso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metso), and [Nordea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordea).[^cite_note-208] As of 2022, the unemployment rate was 6.8%.[^cite_note-209]

As of 2024, 47% of households consisted of a single person, 32% two persons and 21% three or more persons.[^cite_note-210] The average residential space was 40 square metres (430 sq ft) per person.[^cite_note-211] In 2023, Finland's GDP reached €273 billion.[^cite_note-statista-199] In 2022, altogether 74 per cent of employed persons worked in services and administration, 21 per cent in industry and construction, and four per cent in agriculture and forestry.[^cite_note-212]

In 2018, Finland had the highest concentration of [cooperatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative) relative to its population.[^cite_note-213] The largest retailer, which is also the largest private employer, [S-Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S_Group), and the largest bank, [OP Financial Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OP_Financial_Group), in Finland are both cooperatives.

### Energy

Main article: [Nuclear power in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Finland)

See also: [Energy in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Finland), [Nordic energy market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_energy_market), [Peat energy in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peat_energy_in_Finland), and [Nuclear power in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Finland)



The [Olkiluoto Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olkiluoto_Nuclear_Power_Plant). Finland has five commercial nuclear reactors.

[^cite_note-STT2023-214]

The free and largely privately owned financial and physical [Nordic energy markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_energy_market) traded in [NASDAQ OMX Commodities Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASDAQ_OMX_Commodities_Europe) and [Nord Pool Spot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nord_Pool_Spot) exchanges, have provided competitive prices compared with other EU countries. As of 2022, Finland has the lowest non-household electricity prices in the EU.[^cite_note-215]

In 2021, the energy market was around 87 terawatt hours and the peak demand around 14 [gigawatts](21347693.md) in winter.[^cite_note-TilastokeskusEnergia-216][^cite_note-217] Industry and construction consumed 43.5% of total consumption, a relatively high figure reflecting Finland's industries.[^cite_note-TilastokeskusEnergia-216] Finland's [hydrocarbon resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrocarbon_resources) are limited to [peat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peat) and wood. About 18% of the electricity is produced by [hydropower](14073.md)[^cite_note-TilastokeskusEnergia-216] In 2021, [renewable energy](25784.md) (mainly hydropower and various forms of wood energy) was high at 43% compared with the EU average of 22% in final energy consumption.[^cite_note-218] About 20% of electricity is imported, especially from Sweden due to its lower cost there.[^cite_note-219] As of February 2022, Finland's [strategic petroleum reserves](4478230.md) held 200 days worth of net oil imports in the case of emergencies.[^cite_note-220]

Finland has five privately owned nuclear reactors producing 40% of the country's energy.[^cite_note-STT2023-214]

There are three reactors at the [Olkiluoto Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olkiluoto_Nuclear_Power_Plant) and two old Soviet-designed [VVER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VVER)-440 at the [Loviisa Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loviisa_Nuclear_Power_Plant).

The [Onkalo spent nuclear fuel repository](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onkalo_spent_nuclear_fuel_repository) is under construction at the [Olkiluoto Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olkiluoto_Nuclear_Power_Plant) in the municipality of [Eurajoki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurajoki), on the west coast of Finland, by the company [Posiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posiva).[^cite_note-221]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Finland)



A [VR Class Sr2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VR_Class_Sr2) locomotive. The state-owned [VR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VR_\(company\)) operates a railway network serving all major cities in Finland.



A [Finnair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnair) airplane in [Helsinki Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Airport)

Finland's road system is utilized by most internal cargo and passenger traffic. The annual state operated road network expenditure of around €1 billion is paid for with vehicle and fuel taxes which amount to around €1.5 billion and €1 billion, respectively. Among the [Finnish highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways_in_Finland), the most significant and busiest main roads include the [Turku Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku_Highway) ([E18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E18)), the [Tampere Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere_Highway) ([E12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E12)), the [Lahti Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahti_Highway) ([E75](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E75)), and the ring roads ([Ring I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_I) and [Ring III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_III)) of the Helsinki metropolitan area and the [Tampere Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere_Ring_Road) of the [Tampere urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere_urban_area).[^cite_note-222]

The main international passenger gateway is [Helsinki Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Airport), which handled about 15.3 million passengers in 2023.[^cite_note-dom-223] Another [26 airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Finland) have scheduled passenger services.[^cite_note-finavia_pass_stats-224] The Helsinki Airport-based [Finnair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnair), [Blue1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue1), and [Nordic Regional Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Regional_Airlines), [Norwegian Air Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Air_Shuttle) sell air services both domestically and internationally.

The Government annually spends around €350 million to maintain the 5,865-kilometre-long (3,644 mi) network of railway tracks. Rail transport is handled by the state-owned [VR Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VR_Group).[^cite_note-ljvr-225] Finland's first railway was opened in 1862,[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] and today it forms part of the [Finnish Main Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Main_Line), which is more than 800 kilometers long. Helsinki opened the world's northernmost [metro system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Metro) in 1982.

The majority of international cargo shipments are handled at ports. [Vuosaari Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuosaari_Harbour) in Helsinki is the largest container port in Finland; others include [Kotka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kotka), [Hamina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamina), [Hanko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanko,_Finland), [Pori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pori), [Rauma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rauma,_Finland), and [Oulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oulu). There is passenger traffic from Helsinki and Turku, which have ferry connections to [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn), [Mariehamn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariehamn), [Stockholm](26741.md), and [Travemünde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travem%C3%BCnde). The Helsinki-Tallinn route is one of the busiest passenger sea routes in the world.[^cite_note-busiest-228]

### Industry

Main article: [Economy of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Finland)



The [Oasis of the Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasis_of_the_Seas) was built at the [Perno shipyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perno_shipyard) in [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku).

Finland rapidly industrialized after World War II, achieving GDP per capita levels comparable to that of Japan or the UK at the beginning of the 1970s. Initially, most of the economic development was based on two broad groups of export-led industries, the "metal industry" (*metalliteollisuus*) and "forest industry" (*metsäteollisuus*). The "metal industry" includes shipbuilding, metalworking, the [automotive industry](163778.md), engineered products such as motors and [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry), and production of metals and alloys including [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel), [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper), and [chromium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromium). Many of the world's biggest [cruise ships](314855.md), including [MS Freedom of the Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS_Freedom_of_the_Seas) and the [Oasis of the Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasis_of_the_Seas) have been built in Finnish shipyards.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] The "forest industry" includes forestry, timber, pulp and paper, and is often considered a logical development based on Finland's extensive forest resources, as 73% of the area is covered by forest. In the [pulp and paper industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_and_paper_industry), many major companies are based in Finland; [Ahlstrom-Munksjö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahlstrom-Munksj%C3%B6), [Metsä Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mets%C3%A4_Board), and [UPM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UPM_\(company\)) are all Finnish forest-based companies with revenues exceeding €1 billion. However, in recent decades, the Finnish economy has diversified, with companies expanding into fields such as electronics ([Nokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia)), metrology ([Vaisala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisala)), petroleum ([Neste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neste)), and [video games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_Finland) ([Rovio Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rovio_Entertainment)), and is no longer dominated by the two sectors of metal and forest industry. Likewise, the structure has changed, with the service sector growing. Despite this, production for export is still more prominent than in Western Europe, thus making Finland possibly more vulnerable to global economic trends.

In 2023, the Finnish economy was estimated to consist of approximately 2.3% agriculture, 23.9% manufacturing, and 61.4% services.[^cite_note-231] In 2019, the per-capita income of Finland was estimated to be \$48,869. In 2020, Finland was ranked 20th on the [ease of doing business index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business_index), among 190 jurisdictions.

### Public policy

See also: [Nordic model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_model)



Flags of the [Nordic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries) from left to right: Denmark, Iceland, Sweden, Norway and Finland.

Finnish politicians have often emulated the Nordic model.[^cite_note-nordicmodel-146] Nordics have been free-trading for over a century. The level of protection in commodity trade has been low, except for agricultural products.[^cite_note-nordicmodel-146] Finland is ranked 13th in the 2025 global [Index of Economic Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom) and ninth in Europe.[^cite_note-freedom-232] According to the OECD, only four [EU-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU-15) countries have less regulated [product markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Product_market) and only one has less regulated [financial markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_market).[^cite_note-nordicmodel-146] The 2024 [IMD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Management_Development) World Competitiveness Yearbook ranked Finland 15th most [competitive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competitiveness).[^cite_note-233] The [World Economic Forum](172185.md) 2019 index ranked Finland the eleventh most competitive.[^cite_note-234]

The legal system is clear and business bureaucracy less than most countries.[^cite_note-freedom-232] Property rights are well protected and contractual agreements are strictly honoured.[^cite_note-freedom-232] Finland is rated the least corrupt country in the world in the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index)[^cite_note-235] and 20th in the [Ease of doing business index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business_index).[^cite_note-236]

In Finland, [collective labour agreements](12728750.md#Finland) are universally valid. These are drafted every few years for each profession and seniority level, with only a few jobs outside the system. The agreement becomes universally enforceable provided that more than 50% of the employees support it, in practice by being a member of a relevant trade union. The unionization rate is high (70%), especially in the middle class ([AKAVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKAVA), mostly for university-educated professionals: 80%).[^cite_note-nordicmodel-146]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Finland)



The historical [Häme Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A4me_Castle) in [Hämeenlinna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A4meenlinna) is located close to the [Lake Vanajavesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Vanajavesi).

[^cite_note-237]

In 2017, tourism in Finland grossed approximately €15.0 billion. Of this, €4.6 billion (30%) came from foreign tourism.[^cite_note-Matkailutilinpito-238] In 2017, there were 15.2 million overnight stays of domestic tourists and 6.7 million overnight stays of foreign tourists.[^cite_note-TEM-239] Tourism contributes roughly 2.7% to Finland's GDP.[^cite_note-Tourism_infographic-240]

[Lapland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapland_\(Finland\)) has the highest tourism consumption of any Finnish region.[^cite_note-Tourism_infographic-240] Above the [Arctic Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Circle), in midwinter, there is a [polar night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_night), a period when the sun does not rise for days or weeks, or even months, and correspondingly, [midnight sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_sun) in the summer, with no sunset even at midnight (for up to 73 consecutive days, at the northernmost point). Lapland is so far north that the [aurora borealis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora_borealis), [fluorescence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorescence) in the high atmosphere due to [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind), is seen regularly in the fall, winter, and spring. Finnish Lapland is also locally regarded as the home of [Santa Claus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Claus), with several theme parks, such as [Santa Claus Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Claus_Village) and [Santa Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Park) in [Rovaniemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rovaniemi).[^cite_note-travel+leisure-16][^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] Other significant tourist destinations in Lapland also include [ski resorts](179821.md) (such as [Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi,_Finland), [Ruka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruka,_Finland) and [Ylläs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yll%C3%A4s))[^cite_note-travel+leisure-16][^cite_note-243] and [sleigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleigh) rides led by either [reindeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reindeer) or [huskies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husky).[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245]

Tourist attractions in Finland include the natural landscape found throughout the country as well as urban attractions. Finland contains 40 [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Finland) (such as [Koli National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koli_National_Park) in [North Karelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Karelia)), from the Southern shores of the [Gulf of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Finland) to the high [fells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fell) of Lapland. Outdoor activities range from [Nordic skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_skiing), golf, fishing, [yachting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yachting), lake cruises, hiking, and [kayaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayaking), among many others. [Bird-watching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird-watching) is popular for those fond of avifauna, however, [hunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunting) is also popular.

The most famous [tourist attractions in Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tourist_attractions_in_Helsinki) include the [Helsinki Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Cathedral) and the [Suomenlinna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suomenlinna) sea fortress.[^cite_note-travel+leisure-16] The most well-known Finnish amusement parks include [Linnanmäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linnanm%C3%A4ki) in Helsinki and [Särkänniemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A4rk%C3%A4nniemi) in [Tampere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere).[^cite_note-246] [St. Olaf's Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Olaf's_Castle) (*Olavinlinna*) in [Savonlinna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savonlinna) hosts the annual [Savonlinna Opera Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savonlinna_Opera_Festival),[^cite_note-247] and the [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval) milieus of the cities of [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku), [Rauma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rauma,_Finland) and [Porvoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porvoo) also attract spectators.[^cite_note-travel+leisure-16][^cite_note-248] [Commercial cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Sea_cruiseferries) between major coastal and port cities in the Baltic region play a significant role in the local tourism industry.

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Finland)

Population by continent\
of origin (2025)

[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1]

[^cite_note-249]

1. Finland (88.3%)

2. Rest of Europe (5.60%)

3. Asia (4.20%)

4. [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora_in_Finland) (1.40%)

5. Americas (0.30%)

6. Oceania (0.10%)

The population of Finland is approximately 5.7 million. The [birth rate](515898.md) is 7.8 per 1,000 inhabitants, giving a [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) of 1.26 children born per [woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Finland),[^cite_note-immigration-record-high-in-finland-in-2023-250] one of the lowest in the world and well below the replacement rate of 2.1. In 1887, Finland recorded its highest rate, with 5.17 children born per woman.[^cite_note-251] The [child mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_mortality) rate is 2.1 deaths per every 1,000 births, one of the lowest in the world; this is attributed to a strong medical system and robust maternal care initiatives, including the [maternity package programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternity_package).[^cite_note-252] Finland has one of the oldest populations in the world, with a median age of 44.1 years and 23.8% of the population aged 65 and over.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] Finland has an average population density of 19 inhabitants per square kilometre.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] This is the third lowest population density in Europe, after Norway and [Iceland](14531.md), and the lowest in the European Union. Finland's population has always been concentrated in the southern parts of the country, a phenomenon that became more pronounced during the urbanisation of the 20th century. Three of Finland's four largest cities are located in the [Helsinki Metropolitan Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Metropolitan_Area): Helsinki, [Espoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espoo) and [Vantaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vantaa).[^cite_note-253] The city of [Tampere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere) is third after Helsinki and Espoo, and Vantaa is fourth. Other cities with more than 100,000 inhabitants are [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku), [Oulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oulu), [Jyväskylä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyv%C3%A4skyl%C3%A4), [Kuopio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuopio), and [Lahti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahti).

[Finland's immigrant population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Finland) is growing.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] As of 2025, there were 660,800 people with a foreign background living in Finland (11.7% of the population), most of them from Ukraine, Estonia, Iraq, Somalia, China and India.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] The children of foreigners are not automatically granted Finnish citizenship, as Finnish citizenship law maintains a policy of *[jus sanguinis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jus_sanguinis)*, whereby only children born to at least one Finnish parent are granted citizenship. If they are born in Finland and cannot obtain citizenship of another country, they become Finnish citizens.[^cite_note-254] In addition, certain people of Finnish descent living in countries that were once part of the Soviet Union retain the [right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_return#Finland), i.e. the right to establish permanent residence in the country, which would eventually entitle them to citizenship.[^cite_note-255] As of 2025, 10.9% of the Finnish population were foreign born. As a result of recent immigration, the country has significant populations of ethnic [Estonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonians_in_Finland), [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Finland), [Ukrainians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainians_in_Finland), [Iraqis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqis_in_Finland), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people_in_Finland), [Somalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalis_in_Finland), [Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipinos_in_Finland), [Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indians_in_Finland), and [Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Iranians).[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1]

Finland's national minorities include the Sámi, [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people), [Jews](25955086.md), and [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Tatars). The Roma of the [Finnish Kale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Roma) group settled in the country at the end of the 16th century.[^cite_note-256]

### Languages

Main articles: [Finnish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language), [Finland Swedish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_Swedish), and [Languages of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Finland)

See also: [List of municipalities of Finland in which Finnish is not the sole official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_of_Finland_in_which_Finnish_is_not_the_sole_official_language)



Municipalities of Finland:&#x20;

&#x20; unilingually Finnish

&#x20; bilingual with Finnish as majority language, Swedish as minority language

&#x20; bilingual with Swedish as majority language, Finnish as minority language

&#x20; unilingually Swedish

&#x20; bilingual with Finnish as majority language, Sami as minority language

[Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language) and [Swedish](26689.md) are the official languages of Finland. Finnish predominates nationwide while Swedish is spoken in some coastal areas in the west and south (with towns such as [Ekenäs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eken%C3%A4s,_Finland),[^cite_note-257] [Pargas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pargas),[^cite_note-auto1-258] [Närpes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A4rpes),[^cite_note-auto1-258] [Kristinestad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristinestad),[^cite_note-259] [Jakobstad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakobstad)[^cite_note-260] and [Nykarleby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nykarleby).[^cite_note-261]) and in the autonomous region of [Åland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85land), which is the only monolingual Swedish-speaking region in Finland.[^cite_note-262] As of 2025, the [native language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_language) of 83.5% of the population was Finnish,[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] which is part of the Finnic subgroup of the [Uralic language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_language_family). The language is one of only four official [EU languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_European_Union) not of [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) origin, and has no relation through descent to the other [national languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Germanic_languages) of the [Nordics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries). Conversely, Finnish is closely related to [Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language) and [Karelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelian_language), and more distantly to [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language) and the [Sámi languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_languages).

Swedish is the native language of 5.0% of the population ([Swedish-speaking Finns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish-speaking_population_of_Finland)).[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] Swedish is a compulsory school subject and general knowledge of the language is good among many non-native speakers.[^cite_note-263] Likewise, a majority of Swedish-speaking non-Ålanders can speak Finnish.[^cite_note-264] The Finnish side of the land border with Sweden is unilingually Finnish-speaking. The Swedish across the border is distinct from the Swedish spoken in Finland. There is a sizeable pronunciation difference between the varieties of Swedish spoken in the two countries, although their [mutual intelligibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_intelligibility) is nearly universal.[^cite_note-265]

[Finnish Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Kalo_language) is spoken by some 5,000–6,000 people; there are 13,000-14,000 Romani people in Finland.[^cite_note-266] Romani and [Finnish Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Sign_Language) are also recognized in the constitution. There are two sign languages: Finnish Sign Language, spoken natively by 4,000–5,000 people,[^cite_note-267] and [Finland-Swedish Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland-Swedish_Sign_Language), spoken natively by about 150 people. [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishar_Tatar_dialect) is spoken by a [Finnish Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Tatars) minority of about 800 people whose ancestors moved to Finland mainly between the 1870s and 1920s.[^cite_note-268]

The [Sámi languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_languages) have an official status in parts of Lapland, where the [Sámi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_people#Finland), numbering over 10,000[^cite_note-269] are recognized as an [indigenous people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples). About a quarter of them speak a Sámi language as their mother tongue.[^cite_note-Population_2006-12-31-270] The Sámi languages that are spoken in Finland are [Northern Sami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Sami), [Inari Sami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inari_S%C3%A1mi_language), and [Skolt Sami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skolt_Sami_language).[^cite_note-271] The rights of minority groups (in particular Sami, Swedish speakers, and Romani people) are protected by the constitution.[^cite_note-272] The [Nordic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries#Languages) languages and [Karelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelian_language) are also specially recognized in parts of Finland.

As of 2025, the most common foreign languages are [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) (1.8%), [Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language) (0.9%), [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language) (0.8%), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) (0.8%), and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) (0.7%).[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1]

English is studied by most pupils as a compulsory subject from the first grade (at seven years of age), formerly from the third or fifth grade, in the comprehensive school (in some schools other languages can be chosen instead).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] German, French, Spanish and Russian can be studied as second foreign languages from the fourth grade (at 10 years of age; some schools may offer other options).[^cite_note-277]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Finland)

Registered members of religions groups in Finland by the Statistics Finland (2025)

2. Orthodox Church (1.00%)

3. Other Christian (1.00%)
