5. Other Religions (0.40%)

6. Unaffiliated (35.9%)

With 3.5 million members,[^cite_note-279] the [Evangelical Lutheran Church of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_of_Finland) is Finland's largest religious body; As of 2025, 61.2% of Finns were members of the church.[^cite_note-statistics-finland-population-2025-1] The Evangelical Lutheran Church of Finland has seen its share of the country's population declining by roughly one percent annually in recent years. The decline has been due to both church membership resignations and falling baptism rates.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] The second largest group, accounting for 35.9% of the population, has no religious affiliation. A small minority belongs to the [Finnish Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Orthodox_Church) (1.0%). Other [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) denominations and the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Finland) are significantly smaller, as are the [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Finland) and other non-Christian communities (totalling 1.6%). The [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) estimated the [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Finland) population at 2.7% in 2016.[^cite_note-282]

Finland's state church was the Church of Sweden until 1809. As an autonomous Grand Duchy under Russia from 1809 to 1917, Finland retained the Lutheran State Church system, and the Evangelical Lutheran Church of Finland was established. After Finland had gained independence in 1917, religious freedom was declared in the constitution of 1919, and a separate law on religious freedom in 1922. Through this arrangement, the Evangelical Lutheran Church of Finland gained a constitutional status as a national church alongside the Finnish Orthodox Church, whose position however is not codified in the constitution. The main Lutheran and Orthodox churches have special roles such as in state ceremonies and schools.[^cite_note-283]

In 2016, 69.3% of Finnish newborn children were [baptized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism),[^cite_note-284] but by 2019, this figure had declined to around 62%[^cite_note-285]; 82.3% of children aged 15 were [confirmed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_\(Lutheran_Church\)) in 2012 [^cite_note-286] and over 90% of the funerals are Christian. However, the majority of Lutherans attend church only for special occasions like Christmas ceremonies, weddings, and funerals. The Lutheran Church estimates that approximately 1.8% of its members attend church services weekly.[^cite_note-287] The average number of church visits per year by church members is approximately two.[^cite_note-state2004-288]

According to a 2010 [Eurobarometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurobarometer) poll, 33% of Finnish citizens responded that they "believe there is a God"; 42% answered that they "believe there is some sort of spirit or life force"; and 22% that they "do not believe there is any sort of spirit, God, or life force".[^cite_note-EUROBAROMETER-289] According to ISSP survey data (2008), 8% consider themselves "highly religious", and 31% "moderately religious". In the same survey, 28% reported themselves as "agnostic" and 29% as "non-religious".[^cite_note-290]

### Health

Main article: [Healthcare in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Finland)



A man donating blood at [Finnish Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Red_Cross) [Blood Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_Service?action=edit\&redlink=1)

[Life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) was 79 years for men and 84.2 years for women in 2023.[^cite_note-291] The under-five mortality rate was 2.3 per 1,000 live births in 2022, ranking Finland's rate among the lowest in the world.[^cite_note-292] The [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) in 2023 stood at 1.26 children born/per woman and has been below [the replacement rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility) of 2.1 since 1969.[^cite_note-stat.fi-293] With a low birth rate women also become mothers at a later age, the mean age at first live birth being 30.3 in 2023.[^cite_note-stat.fi-293] A 2011 study published in *[The Lancet](463899.md)* medical journal found that Finland had the lowest [stillbirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stillbirth) rate out of 193 countries.[^cite_note-294]

There has been a slight increase or no change in welfare and health inequalities between population groups in the 21st century. Lifestyle-related diseases are on the rise. More than half a million Finns suffer from [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes), [type 1 diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_1_diabetes) being globally the most common in Finland. Many children are diagnosed with [type 2 diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_2_diabetes). The number of [musculoskeletal diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musculoskeletal_disease) and [cancers](105219.md) are increasing, although the cancer prognosis has improved. Allergies and dementia are also growing health problems in Finland. One of the most common reasons for work disability are due to mental disorders, in particular [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_depressive_disorder).[^cite_note-295] Without [age standardization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_standardization), the suicide rates were 13 per 100 000 in 2015, close to the North European average.[^cite_note-296] Age-standardized suicide rates are still among the highest among developed countries in the OECD.[^cite_note-297]

There are 307 residents for each doctor.[^cite_note-298] About 19% of health care is funded directly by households and 77% by taxation.

In April 2012, Finland was ranked second in Gross National Happiness in a report published by The Earth Institute.[^cite_note-299] Since 2012, Finland has every time ranked at least in the top 5 of world's happiest countries in the annual [World Happiness Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Happiness_Report) by the [United Nations](31769.md),[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] as well as ranking as the happiest country since 2018.[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

### Education and science

Main article: [Education in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Finland)



[Helsinki Central Library Oodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Central_Library_Oodi) was chosen as the best new [public library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_library) in the world in 2019.

[^cite_note-305]

Most pre-tertiary education is arranged at the municipal level. Around 3 percent of students are enrolled in private schools (mostly specialist language and international schools).[^cite_note-306] Formal education is usually started at the age of 7. Primary school takes normally six years and lower secondary school three years.

The curriculum is set by the [Ministry of Education and Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_and_Culture_\(Finland\)) and the Education Board. Education is compulsory between the ages of 7 and 18. After lower secondary school, graduates may apply to trade schools or [gymnasiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)) (upper secondary schools). Trade schools offer a [vocational education](56551.md): approximately 40% of an age group choose this path after the lower secondary school.[^cite_note-307] Academically oriented gymnasiums have higher entrance requirements and specifically prepare for [Abitur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abitur) and tertiary education. Graduation from either formally qualifies for tertiary education.



[Linus Torvalds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linus_Torvalds), the Finnish [software engineer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software_engineering) best known for creating the popular [open-source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open-source_software) kernel [Linux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linux).

In tertiary education, two mostly separate and non-interoperating sectors are found: the profession-oriented polytechnics and the research-oriented universities. Education is free and living expenses are to a large extent financed by the government through [student benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_benefit). There are 15 universities and 24 Universities of Applied Sciences (UAS) in the country.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309] The [University of Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Helsinki) is ranked 117th in the Top University Ranking of 2025.[^cite_note-310] [Other reputable universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Finland) of Finland include [Aalto University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aalto_University) in [Espoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espoo), both [University of Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Turku) and [Åbo Akademi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85bo_Akademi_University) in [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku), [University of Jyväskylä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Jyv%C3%A4skyl%C3%A4), [University of Oulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Oulu), [LUT University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LUT_University) in [Lappeenranta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lappeenranta) and [Lahti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahti), [University of Eastern Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Eastern_Finland) in [Kuopio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuopio) and [Joensuu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joensuu), and [Tampere University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere_University).[^cite_note-311]

The [World Economic Forum](172185.md) ranks Finland's tertiary education No. 2 in the world.[^cite_note-312] Around 33% of residents have a tertiary degree, similar to Nordics and more than in most other OECD countries except Canada (44%), United States (38%) and Japan (37%).[^cite_note-313] In addition, 38% of Finland's population has a university or [college degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_degree), which is among the highest percentages in the world.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] Adult education appears in several forms, such as secondary evening schools, civic and workers' institutes, study centres, vocational course centres, and [folk high schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_high_school).[^cite_note-LOC-189]

More than 30% of tertiary graduates are in science-related fields. Forest improvement, materials research, environmental sciences, neural networks, low-temperature physics, brain research, biotechnology, genetic technology, and communications showcase fields of study where Finnish researchers have had a significant impact.[^cite_note-innovation-316] Finland is highly productive in scientific research. In 2005, Finland had the fourth most scientific publications per capita of the OECD countries.[^cite_note-317] In 2007, 1,801 patents were filed in Finland.[^cite_note-318]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Finland)

### Literature

Main articles: [Finnish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_literature) and [Finland-Swedish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland-Swedish_literature)



Writer and artist [Tove Jansson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tove_Jansson)

Written Finnish can be said to have existed since [Mikael Agricola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikael_Agricola) translated the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) into Finnish during the [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) in the 16th century. However, few notable works of literature were produced until the 19th century, which marked the emergence of a Finnish national [Romantic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism). This period prompted [Elias Lönnrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elias_L%C3%B6nnrot) to collect Finnish and Karelian folk poetry and to arrange and publish them as the *[Kalevala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalevala)*, the Finnish [national epic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_epic). The era saw a rise of poets and novelists writing in Finnish, most notably the national writer [Aleksis Kivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksis_Kivi) (*[The Seven Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seven_Brothers)*), as well as [Minna Canth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minna_Canth), [Eino Leino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eino_Leino), and [Juhani Aho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juhani_Aho), who was nominated for the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) twelve times.[^cite_note-319]

Alongside Finnish-language literature, many authors of the national awakening wrote in Swedish. Prominent among them were the national poet [Johan Ludvig Runeberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_Ludvig_Runeberg) (*[The Tales of Ensign Stål](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tales_of_Ensign_St%C3%A5l)*) and [Zachris Topelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zachris_Topelius), whose works occupy a central place in Finland-Swedish literature.

Following Finland's independence, modernist tendencies gained prominence. This development was exemplified by the Finland-Swedish poet [Edith Södergran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edith_S%C3%B6dergran), while Finnish-language authors increasingly explored national, historical, and social themes. Key figures of the early and mid-20th century include [Frans Eemil Sillanpää](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frans_Eemil_Sillanp%C3%A4%C3%A4), who was awarded the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) in 1939, the historical novelist [Mika Waltari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mika_Waltari), and [Väinö Linna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A4in%C3%B6_Linna), whose works *[The Unknown Soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unknown_Soldier_\(novel\))* and the *[Under the North Star trilogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_the_North_Star_trilogy)* became central texts of post-war Finnish literature. From the 1950s onwards, Finnish poetry embraced modernism, beginning with [Paavo Haavikko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paavo_Haavikko).

In contemporary Finnish literature, a wide range of genres and styles coexist. Detective fiction and popular literature have enjoyed particular success. At the same time, the Finland-Swedish writer and artist [Tove Jansson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tove_Jansson) has become one of Finland's most internationally recognised authors. Best known as the creator of *[The Moomins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Moomins)*, she is among the most translated Finnish writers, and her work has had a lasting impact on Finnish and international popular culture, extending far beyond literature into visual media, museums, and other cultural forms.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]

### Visual arts, design, and architecture

See also: [Architecture of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Finland) and [Finnish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_art)



[Akseli Gallen-Kallela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akseli_Gallen-Kallela), *[The Defense of the Sampo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Defense_of_the_Sampo)*, 1896, [Turku Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku_Art_Museum)

The visual arts in Finland started to form their characteristics in the 19th century when [Romantic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_nationalism) was rising in autonomous Finland. The best known Finnish painters, [Akseli Gallen-Kallela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akseli_Gallen-Kallela), started painting in a [naturalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) style but moved to national romanticism. Other notable painters of the era include [Pekka Halonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Halonen), [Eero Järnefelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eero_J%C3%A4rnefelt), [Helene Schjerfbeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helene_Schjerfbeck), and [Hugo Simberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Simberg). In the late 20th century, the homoerotic art of Touko Laaksonen, pseudonym [Tom of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_of_Finland), found a worldwide audience.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

Finland's best-known sculptor of the 20th century was [Wäinö Aaltonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C3%A4in%C3%B6_Aaltonen), remembered for his monumental [busts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bust_\(sculpture\)) and sculptures. The works of [Eila Hiltunen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eila_Hiltunen) and [Laila Pullinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laila_Pullinen) exemplify [modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) in sculpture.

Finns have made major contributions to [handicrafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicraft) and [industrial design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_design): among the internationally renowned figures are [Timo Sarpaneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timo_Sarpaneva), [Tapio Wirkkala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapio_Wirkkala) and [Ilmari Tapiovaara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilmari_Tapiovaara). Finnish architecture is famous around the world, and has contributed significantly to several styles internationally, such as [Jugendstil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugendstil) (or [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau)), [Nordic Classicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Classicism), and [functionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionalism_\(architecture\)). Among the top 20th-century Finnish architects to gain international recognition are [Eliel Saarinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliel_Saarinen) and his son [Eero Saarinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eero_Saarinen). Architect [Alvar Aalto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvar_Aalto) is regarded as among the most important 20th-century designers in the world;[^cite_note-324] he helped bring functionalist architecture to Finland, but soon was a pioneer in its development towards an [organic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_architecture) style.[^cite_note-325] Aalto is also famous for his work in furniture, lamps, textiles, and [glassware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glassware), which were usually incorporated into his buildings.

### Music

Main articles: [Music of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Finland) and [Sami music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sami_music)



The [kantele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kantele) is Finland's national and traditional instrument.

#### Folk

Finnish folk music can be divided into Nordic dance music and the older tradition of poem singing, poems from which the national epic, the *[Kalevala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalevala)*, was created. Much of Finland's classical music is influenced by traditional Finnish and Karelian melodies and lyrics, as comprised in the *Kalevala*. In the historical region of [Finnish Karelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Karelia), as well as other parts of Eastern Finland, the old poem singing traditions were preserved better than in the western parts of the country. In the 19th century [Nordic folk dance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_folk_dance_music) largely replaced the kalevaic tradition. Finnish [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) has undergone a [roots revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roots_revival) and has become a part of [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music). The people of northern Finland, Sweden, and Norway, the [Sámi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A1mi_people), are known primarily for highly spiritual songs called [joik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joik).

#### Classical



The Finnish composer [Jean Sibelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Sibelius) (1865–1957) was a significant figure in the history of [classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_classical_music).

The first [Finnish opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_opera) was written by the German-born composer [Fredrik Pacius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fredrik_Pacius) in 1852. Pacius also wrote the music to the poem [*Maamme/Vårt land* (Our Country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maamme), Finland's [national anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_anthem). In the 1890s Finnish nationalism based on the *Kalevala* spread, and [Jean Sibelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Sibelius) became famous for his vocal symphony *[Kullervo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kullervo_\(Sibelius\))*. In 1899 he composed *[Finlandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandia)*, which played an important role in Finland gaining independence. He remains one of Finland's most popular national figures.

Alongside Sibelius, the distinct Finnish style of music was created by [Oskar Merikanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oskar_Merikanto), [Toivo Kuula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toivo_Kuula), [Erkki Melartin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erkki_Melartin), [Leevi Madetoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leevi_Madetoja), and [Uuno Klami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uuno_Klami). Important modernist composers include [Einojuhani Rautavaara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einojuhani_Rautavaara), [Aulis Sallinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aulis_Sallinen), and [Magnus Lindberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnus_Lindberg), among others. [Kaija Saariaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaija_Saariaho) was ranked the world's greatest living composer in a 2019 composers' poll.[^cite_note-326] Many Finnish musicians have achieved international success. Among them are the conductor [Esa-Pekka Salonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esa-Pekka_Salonen), the opera singer [Karita Mattila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karita_Mattila) and the violinist [Pekka Kuusisto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Kuusisto).

#### Popular music

*Iskelmä* (coined directly from the German word *[Schlager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schlager)*, meaning "hit") is a traditional Finnish word for a light popular song. Finnish popular music also includes various kinds of [dance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_music); [tango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tango_music), a style of [Argentine music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Argentina), is also popular.[^cite_note-327] The light music in Swedish-speaking areas has more influences from Sweden. At least a couple of Finnish [polkas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polka) are known worldwide, such as *[Säkkijärven polkka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A4kkij%C3%A4rven_polkka)*[^cite_note-328] and "*[Ievan polkka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ievan_polkka)*".[^cite_note-329]

During the 1970s, [progressive rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_rock) group [Wigwam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigwam_\(Finnish_band\)) and [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll) group [Hurriganes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurriganes) gained respect abroad. The Finnish [punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock) scene produced some internationally acknowledged names including [Terveet Kädet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terveet_K%C3%A4det) in the 1980s. [Hanoi Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanoi_Rocks) was a pioneering [glam rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glam_rock) act.[^cite_note-330] Many Finnish [metal bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_metal_music) have gained international recognition; Finland has been often called the "Promised Land of Heavy Metal" because there are more than 50 metal Bands for every 100,000 inhabitants – more than any other nation in the world.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] Modern Finnish popular music includes a number of prominent [rock musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music_in_Finland), pop musicians, [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) musicians, [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop) performers, and dance music acts.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336]

Finland has won the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) once in 2006 when [Lordi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lordi) won the contest with the song [''Hard Rock Hallelujah''](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Rock_Hallelujah).[^cite_note-337] The Finnish pop artist [Käärijä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%A4%C3%A4rij%C3%A4) also got second place in the contest in 2023 with his worldwide hit song [''Cha Cha Cha''](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha_Cha_Cha_\(K%C3%A4%C3%A4rij%C3%A4_song\)).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

### Cinema and television

Main articles: [Cinema of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Finland) and [Television in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Finland)

See also: [Lists of Finnish films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Finnish_films)

Aki Kaurismäki in 2012

Film director [Aki Kaurismäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aki_Kaurism%C3%A4ki)

In the film industry, notable modern directors include brothers [Mika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mika_Kaurism%C3%A4ki) and [Aki Kaurismäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aki_Kaurism%C3%A4ki), [Dome Karukoski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_Karukoski), [Antti Jokinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antti_Jokinen), [Jalmari Helander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalmari_Helander), and [Renny Harlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renny_Harlin). Some Finnish [drama series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drama_\(film_and_television\)) are internationally known, such as *[Bordertown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordertown_\(Finnish_TV_series\))*.[^cite_note-340]

One of the most internationally successful Finnish films are *[The White Reindeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_White_Reindeer)*, directed by [Erik Blomberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Blomberg) in 1952, which won the [Golden Globe Award for Best Non-English Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Non-English_Language_Film) in [1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Golden_Globes);[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] *[The Man Without a Past](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Man_Without_a_Past)*, directed by Aki Kaurismäki in 2002, which was nominated for the [Academy Award for Best International Feature Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film) in [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/75th_Academy_Awards) and won the [Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Prix_\(Cannes_Film_Festival\)) at the [2002 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Cannes_Film_Festival);[^cite_note-343] and *[The Fencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fencer)*, directed by [Klaus Härö](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klaus_H%C3%A4r%C3%B6) in 2015, which was nominated for the [73rd Golden Globes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/73rd_Golden_Globes) in the Best Non-English Language Film category as a Finnish/German/Estonian co-production.[^cite_note-344] A 2022 action film *[Sisu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisu_\(film\))*, directed by Jalmari Helander, has achieved the status of the most successful Finnish film of all time in the United States, measured at the box office, with revenues of over \$7 million.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347]

In Finland, the most significant films in Finnish history includes *[The Unknown Soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unknown_Soldier_\(1955_film\))* (1955) and *[Here, Beneath the North Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Here,_Beneath_the_North_Star)* (1968), both directed by [Edvin Laine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edvin_Laine).[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349] A 1960 [crime comedy film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_comedy_film) *[Inspector Palmu's Mistake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspector_Palmu's_Mistake_\(film\))*, directed by [Matti Kassila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matti_Kassila), was voted in 2012 the best Finnish film of all time by Finnish film critics and journalists,[^cite_note-350] but the 1984 comedy film *[Uuno Turhapuro in the Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uuno_Turhapuro_in_the_Army)*, the ninth film in [*Uuno Turhapuro* film series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uuno_Turhapuro), remains Finland's most seen domestic film made by Finnish audience.[^cite_note-351]

### Media and communications

Main article: [Mass media in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Finland)



[Sanomatalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanomatalo) houses several offices of newspapers and radio stations.

Today, there are around 200 [newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Finland), 320 popular magazines, 2,100 professional magazines, and 67 commercial radio stations. The largest newspaper is *[Helsingin Sanomat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsingin_Sanomat)*, its circulation being 339,437 as of 2019.[^cite_note-352] [Yle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yle), the Finnish Broadcasting Company, operates five [television channels](178808.md) and thirteen radio channels. Each year, around 12,000 book titles are published.[^cite_note-Media_moves-353]

Thanks to its emphasis on transparency and equal rights, Finland's press has been rated the freest in the world.[^cite_note-354] Worldwide, Finns, along with other Nordic peoples and the Japanese, spend the most time reading newspapers.[^cite_note-355] In regards to [telecommunication infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Finland), Finland is ranked third, below the United States and Singapore in the Portulan Institute's [Network Readiness Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_Readiness_Index) (NRI) – an indicator for determining the development level of a country's information and communication technologies.[^cite_note-356]

### Sauna

Main article: [Finnish sauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_sauna)



A [smoke sauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoke_sauna) in [Ruka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruka,_Finland), Kuusamo

The Finns' love for [saunas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_sauna) is generally associated with Finnish cultural tradition.[^cite_note-357] Sauna is a type of dry steam bath practiced widely in Finland, which is especially evident in the strong tradition around [Midsummer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midsummer) and [Christmas](6237.md). The word sauna is of Proto-Finnish origin (found in Finnic and Sámi languages) dating back 7,000 years.[^cite_note-358] Steam baths have been part of European tradition elsewhere as well, but the sauna has survived best in Finland, in addition to Sweden, Estonia, Latvia, Russia, Norway, and parts of the United States and Canada. Moreover, nearly all Finnish houses have either their own sauna or in multi-story apartment houses, a timeshare sauna. Municipal swimming halls and hotels have often their own saunas. The [Finnish sauna culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_sauna_culture) is inscribed on the [UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage Lists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists).[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Finnish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_cuisine)



*[Ruisleipä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruisleip%C3%A4)*, a dark [sourdough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sourdough) [rye bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rye_bread), holds the status of the [national food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_food) in Finland.

[^cite_note-361]

Finnish cuisine generally combines traditional country fare and contemporary style cooking. Potato, meat, and [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_\(food\)) play a prominent role in traditional Finnish dishes. Finnish foods often use [wholemeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wholemeal) products ([rye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rye), [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oat)) and berries (such as [bilberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilberry), [lingonberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingonberry), [cloudberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloudberry), and [sea buckthorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_buckthorn)). Milk and its derivatives like [buttermilk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buttermilk) are commonly used as food and drink. The most popular fish food in Finland is [salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon).[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363]

Finland has the world's second highest per capita consumption of [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee).[^cite_note-364] Milk consumption is also high, at an average of about 112 litres (25 imp gal; 30 US gal), per person, per year.[^cite_note-365]

### Public holidays

Main articles: [Public holidays in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Finland) and [Flag flying days in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_flying_days_in_Finland)

There are several holidays in Finland, of which perhaps the most characteristic of Finnish culture include Christmas (*joulu*), Midsummer (*juhannus*), [May Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vappu) (*vappu*) and [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Finland\)) (*itsenäisyyspäivä*). Of these, Christmas and Midsummer are special in Finland because the actual festivities take place on eves, such as [Christmas Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Eve)[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] and [Midsummer's Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juhannus),[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] while Christmas Day and Midsummer's Day are more consecrated to rest. Other public holidays in Finland are [New Year's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year's_Day), [Epiphany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiphany_\(holiday\)#Finland), [Good Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday), [Easter Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Sunday) and [Easter Monday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Monday), [Ascension Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_Day), [All Saints' Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Saints'_Day), and [Saint Stephen's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Stephen's_Day#Finland). All official holidays in Finland are established by Acts of Parliament.[^cite_note-370]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Finland)

See also: [Finland at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_at_the_Olympics)



Finland's [men's national ice hockey team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_men's_national_ice_hockey_team) is [ranked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IIHF_World_Ranking) as one of the best in the world. The team has won five world championships (1995, 2011, 2019, 2022 and 2026) and one Olympic gold medal (2022).

[^cite_note-371]

[^cite_note-372]

Various sporting events are popular in Finland. [Pesäpallo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pes%C3%A4pallo), the Finnish equivalent of American [baseball](3850.md), is the [national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport) of Finland,[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] although the most popular sport in terms of spectators is [ice hockey](14790.md).[^cite_note-375] Other popular sports include [athletics](194904.md), [cross-country skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-country_skiing_\(sport\)), [ski jumping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ski_jumping), [football](10568.md), [volleyball](32558.md), and [basketball](3921.md).[^cite_note-376] Association football is the most played team sport in terms of the number of players in the country.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] [Finland's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_national_basketball_team) has received widespread public attention.[^cite_note-379]

In terms of medals and gold medals won per capita, Finland is the best-performing country in Olympic history.[^cite_note-380] Finland first participated as a nation in its own right at the [Olympic Games](22576.md) in 1908. At the [1912 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1912_Summer_Olympics), three gold medals were won by the original "[Flying Finn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Finn)" [Hannes Kolehmainen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannes_Kolehmainen). In the 1920s and '30s, Finnish long-distance runners dominated the Olympics, with [Paavo Nurmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paavo_Nurmi) winning a total of nine Olympic gold medals and setting 22 official world records between 1921 and 1931. Nurmi is often considered the greatest Finnish sportsman and one of the greatest athletes of all time. The [1952 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Summer_Olympics) were held in Helsinki.

The [javelin throw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javelin_throw) event has brought Finland nine Olympic gold medals, five world championships, five European championships, and 24 world records. Finland also has a notable history in [figure skating](11152.md). Finnish skaters have won 8 world championships and 13 junior world cups in synchronized skating.

Finnish competitors have achieved significant success in [motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorsport). In the [World Rally Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Rally_Championship), Finland has produced eight [world champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Rally_Championship_Drivers'_champions), more than any other country.[^cite_note-381] In [Formula One](10854.md), Finland has won the most [world championships](3437295.md) per capita, with [Keke Rosberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keke_Rosberg), [Mika Häkkinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mika_H%C3%A4kkinen) and [Kimi Räikkönen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimi_R%C3%A4ikk%C3%B6nen) all having won the title.[^cite_note-382]

Some of the most popular recreational sports and activities include [Nordic walking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_walking), running, cycling and skiing. [Floorball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floorball) is the most popular youth and workplace sport.[^cite_note-383]
